# W10 results: 30 questions on corpus-2026-11-19

**Score: 24/30 at acting quality** (tier 1: 14/18), scored by the TL implementer agent (Claude) on 2026-11-19. Gate 4 (corpus-2026-11-17, 2026-11-18): 21/30 (tier 1 12/18). #212 hybrid-norerank on corpus-2026-11-17: 21/30 (tier 1 12/18).

The first (blind) read gave 25/30. A second, independent read of ten questions in the PR review (PR #238, issuecomment-7021166143; [docs/NEXT.md](../NEXT.md) item 7) agreed on nine and failed Q19: the `passes_if` leg 'competitor moves aimed at it' is missing and not named as a gap, because the planner scoped every part to Edward Jones. The TL decided Q19 counts as FAIL (2026-11-19), so the score is 24/30; Q19 is tier 3, so tier 1 stays 14/18. One sample per question.

## How this was run

- Snapshot `corpus-2026-11-19` (224 documents, 21,161 passages: `corpus-2026-11-17` plus 18 advisor-headcount passages, #216). Time windows as of 2026-11-17, as Gate 4, so Q26 (90 days) and Q27 (since 2026-01-01) see the same window; the 18 new passages add no document to any window.
- Code: `main` cdcb6ee, run from `tl/217-final-run` at 99ac877 (plan file only on the branch). Pipeline: token budget 40k (five firms 60k), pool 30 (ADR-019); planner `plan-v2` with the `neighbour_phrases` guard, up to 4 parts (ADR-020, #224); answer prompt v4.1 with per-part gaps and newest-first windows (#215, #230); answer `max_tokens` 20000, `max_retries=0`, strict tools, request `strict-1` (#232).
- Model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, two calls per question (planner + answer). Command: `eval_run.py --real --max-calls 60 --as-of 2026-11-17 --baseline evals/baselines/corpus-2026-11-19/report.json`. Mock check first (0 calls): 29 PASS / 1 FAIL (Q19), refusal 5/5, keyed 22/22, all 30 chunk lists identical to the baseline.
- Two attempts: attempt 1 stopped at the Q14 answer call with an account billing rejection (`400 credit balance is too low`, no output). By the TL decision on #217 that is a stop outside the model's work; attempt 2 (`--max-calls 34`) reused Q1-Q13 and Q14's plan from the cache at 0 calls and called the rest. No question was answered twice.
- Scoring: each answer read against its `passes_if` line in `evals/questions.yaml` (would the project lead forward it with his name on it?), one verdict per question, no half credit, blind first: verdicts were written and their sha256 committed (`ca89a9e4e70fb65de02220c84314d3def4ad051fa0605727990c56a44d5e958f`, plan Progress log, commit 2a65128) before any Gate 4 or W10 verdict was opened for comparison. They are kept unchanged in `w10-report.json` (`blind`).
- Citations: 1101 in total; every cited passage is in its question's retrieved set and in the database. The code's number check flagged 7 claims: 5 are dates the checker reads as figures (Q17, Q18 x2, Q24 x2); 2 are real citation misses where the figure is right but sits in a sibling passage (Q1 claim 15 '$118 billion' is in `MS-TR-2026Q1#3/#11`, not the cited `#23`; Q4 claim 9 '$580 million' is in `MER-TRADE-2026-10-14-IN-02#0`, not the cited `#1/#3`).

## Changes against Gate 4

Each change is labelled **retrieval** (the deciding passages changed; chunk ids named), **pipeline** (a W10 answer-path change: planner parts, prompt v4 / v4.1, code gaps, strict tools, the 20000 cap), **model** (the deciding passages were there both times; the model wrote differently) or **scorer reading**.

| Q7 | FAIL -> PASS (borderline) | **Pipeline**: one part per firm (#214/#224) and prompt v4 put the empty cells in `gaps` (MS payout grid, MS advisor training, Merrill deferred pay); 0 `no_citation` flags (Gate 4: three uncited cells). **Retrieval**: RJF payout ranges `RJF-PR-web-affiliation-options#1`, Merrill training `MER-PR-web-advisor-development-program#1/#2`, `BAC-TR-2025Q4#106` now cited. `MS-10K-2025#153` still not retrieved. |

| Q15 | FAIL -> PASS (borderline) | **Retrieval**: revenue composition per model now in the set: `SCHW-10K-2025#130`, `SCHW-PR-2026-10-15-01#12`, `EDJ-10Q-2025Q3#38`, `RJF-10K-2025#696`, `MS-PR-2026-10-14-01#5`. **Pipeline**: a part for 'what drives the difference' (spread vs fee, sweep, cost). Gate 4's named passage `SCHW-10K-2025#27` is still not retrieved. |
| Q25 | FAIL -> PASS (borderline) | **Retrieval**: `RJF-PR-web-affiliation-options#1` (RJF payout ranges) retrieved and cited (#214), MS formulaic payout via `MS-10K-2025#170` / `MS-10Q-2026Q2#20`, brochure disclosures `MER-ADV-2026#406`, `MS-ADV-2026#173`, `RJF-ADV-2026#50`, `SCHW-ADV-2026#42`. **Pipeline**: v4 per-part gaps now list every undisclosed part (14 gaps; #229 had 0 gaps and a 'boundary note' claim). **Model**: behavior `answer` (bank: `gap_with_adjacent`), as accepted by the TL in #213; the Schwab dividend-deck must-not chunks were not retrieved in this run. |

| Q19 | PASS -> FAIL (borderline; second read and TL decision) | **Pipeline**: the planner scoped all four parts to Edward Jones (`firms=['EDJ']`), so competitor moves aimed at younger investors were never searched; only Schwab's Gen Z / millennial share of new households (`SCHW-TR-2026Q3#11`, `SCHW-TR-2025Q3#11`) reached the answer, as adjacent outcomes. Gate 4's missed passage `EDJ-10K-2025#88` is now retrieved and cited, and the hand-off is kept; the missing leg is not named as a gap. |
| Q24 | PASS -> FAIL (borderline) | **Model** (with the **pipeline** part shape): the four five-firm parts give per-firm rows of assets, counts and definitions, but no cross-firm statement that the comparison is invalid and no ratio where both inputs are disclosed. Gate 4 said so in one claim (`EDJ-10Q-2026Q2#61`, `RJF-10K-2025#9`: 'not like-for-like ... different asset measures and dates') and a gap. Both inputs are retrieved and cited here, so retrieval did not cause it. The #230 run (v4.1) was scored PASS with the invalidity answered per firm: a reading difference for the second read. |
| Q26 | PASS -> FAIL (borderline) | **Scorer reading**, not a system change: Gate 4's answer also had no 'why it might matter' line per item and was passed; this blind read applies that part of `passes_if`. **Model**: one inversion (the 2026-11-05 outlook item placed last) and a background item outside the window labelled as such. Window, retrieval of trade press and order across parts otherwise as #230 fixed. |

Unchanged failures: Q11: unchanged FAIL (c, b): `gap_with_adjacent`, definitions only for EDJ and RJF; NEXT item 5. Q20: unchanged FAIL (now b, r): `gap_with_adjacent`; Fidelity next-gen study and UBS sample passages still not retrieved. Q23: unchanged FAIL (c, b): Edward Jones series only; NEXT item 5.

Net: six FAIL -> PASS (Q3, Q7, Q12, Q15, Q25, Q28), three PASS -> FAIL (Q19, Q24, Q26), 21 + 6 - 3 = 24 (the first read had Q19 PASS: 25). Four of the six gains rest on W10 parts and per-part gaps (Q3, Q7, Q12, Q28: the 'part disappears' failures of Gate 4), two on retrieval reaching passages Gate 4 missed (Q15, Q25). Against the W10 partial runs, every question run there keeps its latest W10 verdict except Q24 (PASS in #230) and Q26 (PASS borderline in #230): both are borderline readings of the same answer shape. Q19 was not run in a W10 partial run.

## Cost

| Attempt | Planner calls | Answer calls | Input tokens | Output tokens |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 (`.eval-runs/0217-real`, stopped at Q14's answer) | 14 | 13 + 1 rejected (0 output) | 867,885 | 119,301 |
| 2 (`.eval-runs/0217-real-2`, 27 reused) | 16 | 17 | 1,151,796 | 142,007 |
| Total | 30 | 30 + 1 rejected | 2,019,681 | 261,308 |

61 calls: 60 with an output (30 planner, 30 answer; 60 raw files) and the rejected Q14 answer request. Every output `claude-sonnet-5-5`, `stop_reason` `tool_use`, request `strict-1`; no invalid output, no planner fallback, no output cut (largest answer: Q11, 14,852 output tokens; largest input: Q15, 100,796). Estimated cost at $2 / M input and $10 / M output (the rates used in the ExecPlan): about $6.65. Real input tokens are about 2x the characters / 4 estimate (answer input 1.98M vs ~1.0M estimated).

## The 30 answers

Each answer as the system returned it: claims with the part they answer and the passages they cite (document type, publication date, opening of the stored passage), adjacent evidence, the gaps the answer names and the caveats inserted by code.

### Q1: What is Morgan Stanley's strategy to increase their net new assets?

**Verdict: PASS**. Channels management credits (workplace, E*TRADE, advisors, integrated firm) in its own words, each dated 4Q24-3Q26; results by quarter; target and gaps named.

Tier 1, use case `entity_brief`. Expected behavior `answer`, got `answer`. Retrieval: 134 passages (~40221 tokens) in 4 part(s): p1 MS: Morgan Stanley's stated strategy and priorities for growing net new assets in wealth management.; p2 MS: Channels and initiatives Morgan Stanley uses to attract assets: workplace/stock plan, financial advisor productivity and recruiting, E*TRADE, referrals from Institutional Securities.; p3 MS: Reported net new asset results, pace and management commentary on drivers of flows and targets.; p4 MS: Competition, headwinds or areas where net new asset growth is pressured, such as cash sweep, client cash and competitive pricing..

*Passes if:* It names the channels management itself credits for net new asset growth, in management's framing, with the emphasis each is given -- and it is dated. Strategy stated in 2024 is not strategy stated today, and an answer that blurs the two has failed.

**Claims**

1. [p1] On the 4Q24 earnings call (January 2025), CEO Ted Pick said investments in the self-directed and Workplace channels "drive our differentiated client acquisition funnel." He said the expanded offering reaches over 19 million relationships and that the firm added net new assets of over $250 billion in each of the past two years, "on track to delivering $10 trillion plus of total client assets."
   - `MS-TR-2024Q4#9` (transcript, 2025-01-16): "Ted Pick CEO at Morgan Stanley Amidst geopolitical and interest rate uncertainty, each region grew revenues by roughly 20% in 2024. These results follow multiple years of investment in talent and leadership, as well as e..."

### Q3: What is Schwab's strategy for its RIA custody business?

**Verdict: PASS**. Framing, priorities and competition from calls/10-K/deck; concessions handled: pullbacks (long/short minimums, SAN minimum) cited and a gap says Schwab does not concede ground in its own words. Trade press leads the list, labelled indicative.

Tier 1, use case `entity_brief`. Expected behavior `answer`, got `answer`. Retrieval: 156 passages (~40176 tokens) in 4 part(s): p1 SCHW: Schwab's stated strategy and priorities for its RIA custody business (Advisor Services); p2 SCHW: Competition named and where Schwab is losing ground or pulling back in RIA custody; p3 SCHW: Reported results and metrics for Advisor Services: client assets, net new assets, RIA flows, new advisor wins; p4 SCHW: Offerings, investments and pricing for RIA custody: technology, integration, trading, cash and bank sweep, advisor services economics.

*Passes if:* It describes how Schwab frames the advisor-services business, its stated priorities, and the competitive pressure management acknowledges -- including where they concede ground.

### Q7: How do Morgan Stanley, Merrill and Raymond James each approach advisor recruiting and retention?

**Verdict: PASS** - borderline. Same dimensions for all three (compensation/incentives, training, results, competitive posture); every cell cited; empty cells in gaps (MS payout grid, MS advisor training, Merrill deferred comp). Recruiting posture spread across rows; RJF payout philosophy only as ranges.

Tier 2, use case `head_to_head`. Expected behavior `answer`, got `answer`. Retrieval: 142 passages (~40054 tokens) in 4 part(s): p1 MS: Morgan Stanley's approach to recruiting and retaining financial advisors, including compensation, deferred comp, training, and results.; p2 MER: Merrill's approach to recruiting and retaining financial advisors, including training program, compensation, and results.; p3 RJF: Raymond James's approach to recruiting and retaining financial advisors across employee and independent channels, including affiliation, transition assistance, and results.; p4 MS/MER/RJF: Competitive positioning, advisor movement and recruiting incentives, and areas of pullback or losses at each firm..

### Q8: Compare how Schwab and Raymond James describe their value proposition to independent advisors.

**Verdict: PASS**. Each firm's own language; Schwab custody vs RJF affiliation (IC and RCS) kept as separate models.

Tier 2, use case `head_to_head`. Expected behavior `answer`, got `answer`. Retrieval: 142 passages (~40141 tokens) in 2 part(s): p1 SCHW: How Schwab describes its value proposition to independent advisors (Schwab Advisor Services custody, technology, service, and support).; p2 RJF: How Raymond James describes its value proposition to independent advisors (Independent Contractors channel and RJ Financial Services, and Raymond James Investment Advisors Services)..

*Passes if:* It uses each firm's own language and keeps custody and affiliation distinct rather than flattening two different business models into one comparison.

### Q12: How does Schwab utilize its scale to cheaply acquire new clients and how could Edward Jones replicate a similar strategy?

**Verdict: PASS**. Schwab channels and economics (cost per account, scale cost advantage), EDJ cost structure (FA comp, margin, comms expense) alongside, explicit hand-off of the replication question.

Tier 3, use case `mechanism`. Expected behavior `answer_with_boundary`, got `answer_with_boundary`. ### Q14: How does Raymond James's affiliation-choice model work commercially?

**Verdict: PASS**. Economics per option for advisor (payout ranges, costs borne) and firm (fees, revenue lines, margin effect, recruiting cost) from RJF's own filings and pages; per-option margin gap named.

Tier 3, use case `mechanism`. Expected behavior `answer`, got `answer`. Retrieval: 146 passages (~40261 tokens) in 4 part(s): p1 RJF: What affiliation options (employee, independent contractor, independent RIA custodial) does the firm offer advisors and how are they structured?; p2 RJF: How are advisors paid and how does the firm earn revenue and margin under each affiliation option (payout, fees, asset-based revenue, platform fees)?; p3 RJF: How does the affiliation choice drive recruiting, retention, and advisor growth, including transition assistance and results reported?; p4 RJF: How does management describe the strategy, competitive positioning and shared platform and technology supporting the choice model?.

*Passes if:* It explains what each affiliation option means economically for both the advisor and the firm, sourced to Raymond James's own description rather than to industry commentary about it.

**Claims**

1. [p1] The firm's 2025 10-K (published 2025-11-25) says it offers multiple affiliation options, which it calls AdvisorChoice®. Advisors primarily affiliate directly as employees or independent contractors, or as employees of third-party RIAs and broker-dealers served through the RIA and Custody Services (RCS) division.
   - `RJF-10K-2025#10` (10-K, 2025-11-25): "We offer multiple affiliation options, which we refer to as AdvisorChoice®. Financial advisors primarily affiliate with us directly as either employees or independent contractors or as employees of third-party Registered..."
2. [p1] Employee advisors, per the 2025 10-K, work in a traditional branch supported by local management and administrative staff and serve predominantly retail clients. Their compensation primarily includes a payout on revenues they generate, and they participate in the firm's employee benefit plans.
   - `RJF-10K-2025#10` (10-K, 2025-11-25): "We offer multiple affiliation options, which we refer to as AdvisorChoice®. Financial advisors primarily affiliate with us directly as either employees or independent contractors or as employees of third-party Registered..."
3. [p1] Independent contractor advisors, per the 2025 10-K, are generally responsible for all their direct costs and so receive a higher payout percentage than employee advisors. The firm describes these options as designed to let advisors use as much or as little of its support as they choose. They may affiliate directly or through an affiliated bank or credit union in the Financial Institutions Division.
   - `RJF-10K-2025#11` (10-K, 2025-11-25): "Our financial advisors who are independent contractors are generally responsible for all of their direct costs and, accordingly, receive a higher payout percentage on the revenues they generate than employee financial ad..."
4. [p1] The Raymond James affiliation-options web page (page content as of 2026-04-21) lists five AdvisorChoice options: Traditional Employee, Advisor Select, Independent Contractor, Bank & Credit Union, and Corporate RIA. It also lists RIA & Custody Services as a model. Qualification thresholds differ: for example, Traditional Employee needs a minimum of 5 years of experience, and Independent Contractor needs a minimum of 3 years and a minimum T12 of $150,000 to join an existing branch or $400,000 to open a new one.

**Gaps (named by the answer)**

- [p2] Revenue and margin by affiliation option (employee, independent contractor, RCS) are not disclosed. The firm reports PCG as one segment and shows only RCS assets and the revenue line RCS fees map to ("Account and service fees"). *Would fill it:* Investor-day or segment disclosure breaking out PCG revenue and pre-tax margin by channel; management commentary on channel economics
- [p2] Headline payout ranges are published (company recruiting page, as of 2026-04-21); the full payout grid is not public. *Would fill it:* Raymond James advisor recruiting materials or offer terms; industry compensation surveys
- [p3] Advisor attrition by affiliation option is not disclosed. The firm gives overall advisor counts, notes that RCS advisors are excluded from the counts, and says it no longer reports headcount (per trade press), so recruiting results cannot be set against departures by channel. *Would fill it:* Firm disclosure of advisor departures and retention by channel; 10-K human capital section

### Q15: What drives the difference between how Schwab earns on client assets and how an advisor-led model earns?

**Verdict: PASS** - borderline. Disclosed revenue composition for Schwab and each advisor-led firm; mechanism shown through spread vs fee, cash-sweep and cost-structure rows; breakdowns named (GWIM, no revenue yield). List-like, synthesis left to the reader; SCHW-10K-2025#27 not retrieved.

Tier 3, use case `mechanism`. Expected behavior `answer`, got `answer`. Retrieval: 205 passages (~60225 tokens) in 3 part(s): p1 SCHW: How Schwab earns on client assets: net interest revenue, spread on sweep cash and bank deposits, asset management fees, trading revenue.; p2 MS/EDJ/MER/RJF: How advisor-led firms earn on client assets: fee-based advisory asset-based fees, commissions, bank sweep net interest income, revenue per client asset.; p3 SCHW/MS/EDJ/MER/RJF: What drives the difference: spread-based versus fee-based revenue, cash sweep allocation and rates, advisor compensation payout, pricing and cost structure..

*Passes if:* It uses disclosed revenue composition for each model and explains the structural difference as a mechanism, not as a ratio. Where the comparison breaks down, it says so.

**Claims**

1. [p1] Schwab (2025 10-K, published 2026-02-25): net revenues were $23.9 billion in 2025. Net interest revenue was $11.8 billion, asset management and administration fees $6.5 billion, trading revenue $3.9 billion, and bank deposit account fee revenue $977 million. Schwab ties net interest revenue to lower funding costs and growth in margin and bank lending. It ties asset management and administration fees to higher client asset balances, and trading revenue to higher trading volume.

### Q16: What will receiving financial advice in the future look like for clients?

**Verdict: PASS** - borderline. Positions attributed and dated, no own forecast; mostly organised by position but five 'Firm view' items are organised by firm.

Tier 3, use case `theme_scan`. Expected behavior `answer`, got `answer`. Retrieval: 135 passages (~40014 tokens) in 4 part(s): p1 all: How will clients receive financial advice in the future, and how is advice delivery expected to evolve?; p2 all: How will AI, digital tools and technology change the way clients receive and interact with advice?; p3 all: How will advice become more holistic, personalized and goals-based, and how will advisor roles and fees shift?; p4 all: How will advice be accessed through different channels and by new client segments, such as virtual, remote and mass-affluent clients?.

*Passes if:* It presents the spread of positions organised by position rather than by firm, each attributed and dated, and offers no single forecast of its own.

**Claims**

1. [p1] Position: digital-first, AI-assisted advice becomes the expectation. A Wealthtender study (published 2025-08-13) anticipates continued growth in AI tool use for advisor searches, rising expectations for faster response times and digital-first communications, and further acceptance of virtual advisor relationships regardless of geography. It says the pattern of higher comfort with AI for administrative and analytical tasks than for autonomous decision-making is likely to remain consistent.
   - `WEALTHTENDER-ADVISOR-SEARCH-2025#62` (research, 2025-08-13): "The integration of technology into advisor search and service delivery appears to have accelerated, with consumers now expecting digital-first experiences even for traditional services like financial advice. Q: What tren..."
2. [p2] Position: AI changes advisor capacity and workflows, with the client still dealing with a human. Deloitte (article, 2026-05-20) says advisers spend nearly 70% of their time on behind-the-scenes work. It predicts adviser productivity uplift of roughly 30% to 100% by 2032 from agentic AI, and says this could shift the issue from adviser scarcity to adviser capability.
   - `DELOITTE-AGENTIC-2026#0` (research, 2026-05-20): "The agentic AI productivity wave is heading for wealth management Agentic AI capabilities could help firms lower cost-to-serve, enhance advice quality and delivery, improve adviser and client experience, and drive compet..."
3. [p2] Position: AI is widely expected to be transformative. In Accenture research (published 2025-06-23), 97% say AI will have a transformative effect on the future of financial advice to a moderate or great extent in the next three years. 55% say AI will be most helpful in eliminating administrative tasks, identifying next best action opportunities, and automating marketing and client contact. 47% believe AI will have a direct, measurable and consistent impact on the client-adviser relationship in the next 12 months.
   - `ACCENTURE-GENAI#3` (research, 2025-06-23): "/ 97% / say AI will have a transformative effect on the future of financial advice to a moderate or great extent in the next three years / /---/---/ / Transformation is already underway 47% 40% / believe AI will have a d..."

McKinsey (published 2026-04-08) says AI will aggressively assume specific tasks such as preparation, data extraction and document drafting. It calls the replacement thesis "mostly false" for core advice segments, where nearly 80 percent of affluent households still prefer a human relationship. It adds that an AI takeover might be possible for standardized, lower-touch service models and for younger clients, who are increasingly comfortable with AI-only interfaces.

### Q17: What are these firms saying about AI in advice delivery, and who is claiming the most?

**Verdict: PASS** - borderline. Deployed named tools (Raimond 6,500 users, Rai, Charley, Portfolio Insights, LeadIQ) separated from aspirations and research estimates; 'who claims most' answered only partly (BAC most explicit with figures, Schwab result is developer-only).

Tier 1, use case `theme_scan`. Expected behavior `answer`, got `answer`. Retrieval: 136 passages (~40198 tokens) in 4 part(s): p1 all: How firms describe using artificial intelligence in delivering financial advice to clients (planning, portfolio, client conversations).; p2 all: Claims about AI improving advisor productivity, capacity, meeting preparation, note-taking and practice efficiency.; p3 all: Quantified or strongest claims of AI results, adoption, investment and competitive advantage in advice.; p4 all: How firms see the future of advice delivery with AI: human advisor role, hybrid model, robo-advice competition, risks and compliance..

*Passes if:* It separates specificity from volume: a named tool actually deployed to advisors is a different claim from an aspiration on an earnings call, and the answer distinguishes them rather than treating both as "investing in AI."

### Q18: Where is the industry heading on fee structures and pricing transparency?

**Verdict: PASS**. Research, firm disclosures, regulation and trade press each labelled; disclosed pricing changes (Merrill data fees) kept apart from commentary.

Tier 3, use case `theme_scan`. Expected behavior `answer`, got `answer`. Retrieval: 132 passages (~40138 tokens) in 4 part(s): p1 all: How wealth-management advisory fee structures are shifting (AUM-based fees, flat or retainer fees, hourly, subscription, fee compression).; p2 all: Trends and disclosures on pricing transparency and fee disclosure to clients, including regulatory requirements.; p3 all: Drivers of fee pressure: competition, zero-commission, low-cost providers, robo-advisors, and client demand for value.; p4 all: Changes in product and platform fees: ETF and fund expense ratios, cash sweep revenue, unbundled pricing, and revenue sharing..

*Passes if:* Disclosed pricing changes, regulatory direction and published research are kept distinct from commentary and opinion, each labelled for what it is.

### Q19: How should the preferences of younger investors shape Edward Jones's offerings?

**Verdict: FAIL** (s) - borderline. Cohort evidence dated and sourced and the 'should' hand-off present, but the 'competitor moves aimed at it' leg is missing and not named as a gap: every planner part was scoped to Edward Jones, so no competitor move was searched (Schwab's Gen Z share of new households is an outcome, not a move). First read PASS (borderline); second read FAIL; TL decision 2026-11-19: FAIL.

Retrieval: 75 passages (~23655 tokens) in 4 part(s): p1 EDJ: What preferences, needs and behaviors do younger investors and clients under 40 show (digital access, advice, planning, products, values)?; p2 EDJ: What products, services, and advice offerings does the firm provide or plan for younger clients and next-generation investors?; p3 EDJ: What results does the firm report on acquiring and retaining younger clients, including client age mix, new clients, and generational wealth transfer?; p4 EDJ: How does the firm describe competition for younger investors from digital platforms, robo-advisors and online brokers, and its response?.
